# Language reviews and recordings still needed

Generated by `npx tsx scripts/export-language-reviews.ts --since 5ad5bf7`. Regenerate it after the next
batch of lessons lands rather than editing the tables by hand; the sections at the top are written by hand
in the script and say what is waiting on a person.

## What is waiting, and who does it

1. **Estonian, read by the native speaker** (ADR-005 amendment 6). Every Estonian line the lessons and their
   scenes added or changed since `5ad5bf7`: 1996 lines (A1 1, A2 1995). Section 1 below. Mark
   each OK or write the fix; a fix goes into the lesson or scene file the lesson id names, and must still pass
   `npm test` (every word taught before it is shown).
2. **Recordings, by the native speaker.** The A1 and A2 lessons are only partly recorded: the catalog in
   `lib/audio/native.ts` reaches lessons 1 to 13 of A1, and a man's part plays the native voice only where every
   line of it is recorded. Section 2 lists 1667 lines and words no recording says yet (A1 664, A2 1003),
   men's parts, model answers and taught words. One retake is known and listed first in that section.
3. **Russian and Ukrainian, read by fluent readers.** Both languages are machine translated and say so on
   screen. The lines added for A1 and A2 are in `docs/translations/a1-a2/ru.md` and
   `docs/translations/a1-a2/uk.md`, and every line the app holds is in `docs/translations/ru.md` and
   `docs/translations/uk.md`. `npx tsx scripts/export-translations.ts --csv` writes the same as a sheet, and
   `npm run apply:translation-review` reads a filled one back.
4. **The picture round's example sentences**, read by the native speaker: `docs/picture-examples-for-review.md`.

A note on the scenes' prepared lines: the rows in `lib/scenes/bank/a2-*.ts` carry a `reviewedAt` date, and
nobody has read them yet. They are in section 1 under each role-play lesson, as "Live scene, they say".

### A2: Whose and where

#### A2 1. Whose is it? (`a2-01-whose`)

| # | Where | Estonian | English | OK / fix |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | Conversation, Mari | Tom, kelle telefon see on? | Tom, whose phone is this? | |
| 3 | Conversation, Tom | See ei ole minu telefon. See on Jaani telefon. | That's not my phone. It's Jaan's phone. | |
| 4 | Conversation, Mari | Ja kelle kott see on? | And whose bag is this? | |
| 5 | Conversation, Tom | See on minu sõbra kott. Tema nimi on Peeter. | That's my friend's bag. His name's Peeter. | |
| 6 | Conversation, Mari | Aga see võti? Kas see on sinu võti? | And this key? Is it your key? | |
| 7 | Conversation, Tom | Ei, see on minu venna võti. Ta tuleb homme. | No, it's my brother's key. He's coming tomorrow. | |
| 8 | Conversation, Mari | Hästi. Ja see raamat on minu oma. | All right. And this book is mine. | |
| 9 | Words (word) | oma | own; with {minu}, mine | |
| 10 | Words (form) | sõbra | a friend's, of a friend | |
| 11 | Words (form) | venna | a brother's, of a brother | |
| 12 | Words (form) | õe | a sister's, of a sister | |
| 13 | Words (form) | lapse | a child's, of a child | |
| 14 | Words (form) | mehe | a man's, a husband's | |
| 15 | Words (form) | naise | a woman's, a wife's | |
| 16 | How it works | sõbra kott | The [omastav] (2nd form) says whose: {sõbra kott}, a friend's bag. It goes in front, like 's in English. | |
| 17 | How it works | minu, sinu, nende, mina, sina, nemad | You've said it since A1: {minu}, {sinu} and {nende} are the [omastav] (2nd form) of {mina}, {sina} and {nemad}. | |
| 18 | How it works | Jaan, Jaani telefon, Peeter, Peetri, Mari telefon | A name does it too. {Jaan} becomes {Jaani telefon} and {Peeter} becomes {Peetri}, and a name ending in a vowel stays as it is: {Mari telefon}. | |
| 19 | How it works | Kelle?, Kelle kott see on? | {Kelle?} asks whose: {Kelle kott see on?} | |
| 20 | How it works | Minu oma, See raamat on minu oma | {Minu oma} means mine: {See raamat on minu oma}. | |
| 21 | How it works | sõber, sõbra, laps, lapse, mees, mehe | The [omastav] (2nd form) isn't always the word with a letter on the end: {sõber} is {sõbra}, {laps} is {lapse}, {mees} is {mehe}. Learn it with the word. | |
| 22 | How it works, table | sõbra kott | a friend's bag | |
| 23 | How it works, table | venna võti | a brother's key | |
| 24 | How it works, table | õe raamat | a sister's book | |
| 25 | How it works, table | lapse tuba | a child's room | |
| 26 | How it works, table | mehe auto | a husband's car | |
| 27 | How it works, table | ema telefon | Mom's phone | |
| 28 | Say it, question | Kelle telefon see on? | Whose phone is this? | |
| 29 | Say it, model answer | See on minu telefon. | It's my phone. | |
| 30 | Say it, model answer | Kelle kott see on? | Whose bag is this? | |
| 31 | Say it, question | Kelle võti see on? | Whose key is this? | |
| 32 | Say it, model answer | See on minu venna võti. | It's my brother's key. | |
| 33 | Say it, model answer | See raamat on minu oma. | This book is mine. | |

#### A2 2. The apartment key (`a2-02-which`)

| # | Where | Estonian | English | OK / fix |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 34 | Conversation, Liis | Tere tulemast! See on teie korteri võti. | Welcome! This is your apartment key. | |
| 35 | Conversation, Tom | Aitäh! Ja see väike võti? | Thank you! And this little key? | |
| 36 | Conversation, Liis | See on postkasti võti. | That's the mailbox key. | |
| 37 | Conversation, Tom | Selge. Kus on vannitoa uks? | Got it. Where's the bathroom door? | |
| 38 | Conversation, Liis | Siin. Ja seal on magamistoa uks. | Here. And that's the bedroom door over there. | |
| 39 | Conversation, Tom | Köögi aken on väga suur. Ilus korter! | The kitchen window's really big. A lovely apartment! | |
| 40 | Words (phrase) | Tere tulemast! | Welcome! | |
| 41 | Words (word) | postkast | mailbox | |
| 42 | Words (form) | postkasti | of the mailbox | |

| 47 | Words (form) | elutoa | of the living room | |
| 48 | Words (form) | akna | of the window | |
| 49 | Words (form) | ukse | of the door | |
| 50 | How it works | korteri võti, köögi aken | The [omastav] (2nd form) also says which one: {korteri võti}, the apartment key; {köögi aken}, the kitchen window. And every ending you know is built on it. | |
| 51 | How it works | Toas, toa, köögis, köögi | Look down the last column. {Toas} is {toa} with -s on it, and {köögis} is {köögi} with -s: the endings go on the [omastav] (2nd form), never on the first. | |
| 52 | How it works | vannituba, telefoninumber | Estonian often makes one word of the two: {vannituba} is a bath's room, {telefoninumber} a phone's number. | |
| 53 | How it works | tuba, toa, köök, köögi, aken, akna | A word can change inside: {tuba} is {toa}, {köök} is {köögi}, {aken} is {akna}. Don't guess the [omastav] (2nd form); learn it with the word. | |
| 54 | How it works, table | köögi |  | |
| 55 | How it works, table | korteri |  | |
| 56 | How it works, table | elutoa |  | |
| 57 | Say it, model answer | See on korteri võti. | This is the apartment key. | |
| 58 | Say it, question | Mis võti see on? | What key is this? | |
| 59 | Say it, model answer | See on postkasti võti. | It's the mailbox key. | |
| 60 | Say it, model answer | Kus on vannitoa uks? | Where's the bathroom door? | |

| 250 | How it works | kooli, linna, panka | Into has a long form, -sse, and for many words a short one too, which is what people say: {kooli}, {linna}, {panka}. Learn the short one with the word; where you don't know it, the long one is always understood. | |

| 492 | How it works | kesklinnas, kohvikusse, turul, turule, panga ees, kiriku taga | Count the endings: {kesklinnas} and {kohvikusse} are inside, {turul} and {turule} outside, {panga ees} and {kiriku taga} the [omastav] (2nd form) with a small word after. | |
| 493 | How it works | Kuidas ma turule saan?, saama | {Kuidas ma turule saan?} is how do I get to the market: {saama} is get, as in get somewhere. | |
| 494 | How it works | Pärast, Kas me läheme pärast kohvikusse? | {Pärast} on its own means afterwards: {Kas me läheme pärast kohvikusse?} | |

| 547 | How it works | elan, elasin, elasid, elasime, elasite | The past of most verbs ends in -sin: {elan}, {elasin}. The other persons follow it the way the present's do: {elasid}, {elasime}, {elasite}. | |

| 1269 | How it works | võtan, võtaksin, saan, saaksin | Would is -ks- before the ending: {võtan} and then {võtaksin}, {saan} and then {saaksin}. It turns any ask into a polite one, and it works for every verb you know. A class calls it the [tingiv kõneviis] (-ks, would). | |
| 1270 | How it works | võta, võtaksin, võtaks | Take the [olevik] (I form), drop the -n, and add -ksin, -ksid, -ks, -ksime, -ksite, -ksid: {võta} and then {võtaksin}, {võtaks}. | |
| 1271 | How it works | Kas me võiksime...?, Kas te sooviksite...? | {Kas me võiksime...?} is could we, the softest way to ask for something. A waiter says {Kas te sooviksite...?} | |
| 1272 | How it works | See oleks tore | To be has it too: {See oleks tore} is that would be nice. | |
| 1273 | How it works | Tahaksin, tahaksin | {Tahaksin} is not I wanted. Wanted is the past of part three; would like, now, is {tahaksin}. | |

## 2. Recordings to make

### The known retake

In `a1-elad`, the evening about where you live, Tom answers `Ei, ma ei ela Tallinnas. Ma elan Tartus.`
Everywhere else in the course Tom lives in Tallinn. The line is a native recording, so its text was left
alone. Record the line again with the text the native speaker settles on (for instance Tom living in Tallinn
and somebody else in Tartu), change the lesson to match, and give the new take a new id in `lib/audio/native.ts`.

### Not recorded yet

Conversation lines are listed only for the men's parts (Tom, Jaan, Peeter), since the recordings are one man's
voice and he never plays a woman's part. Add a recorded line to `NATIVE_CLIPS`, raise `NATIVE_CUT`, and add
Peeter to `NATIVE_PARTS` once his lines are in.
